=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a sequence of integers (X_1,\dots,X_M) of length M consisting of 1,\dots,K.
Find the number of sequences (A_1,\dots,A_N) of length N consisting of 1,\dots,K that satisfy the following condition, modulo 998244353:

- Among all sequences of length M consisting of 1,\dots,K, the only sequence that cannot be obtained as a (not necessarily contiguous) subsequence of (A_1,\dots,A_N) is (X_1,\dots,X_M).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M K
X_1 X_2 \dots X_M

Output

Print the number of sequences satisfying the condition, modulo 998244353.

Constraints


- 2\le M,K \le N \le 400
- 1\le X_i \le K
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 2 3
1 1

Sample Output 1

4

The following four sequences satisfy the condition:

- (2, 3, 1, 2, 3)
- (2, 3, 1, 3, 2)
- (3, 2, 1, 2, 3)
- (3, 2, 1, 3, 2)

Sample Input 2

400 3 9
1 8 6

Sample Output 2

417833302

Sample Input 3

29 3 10
3 3 3

Sample Output 3

495293602

Sample Input 4

29 3 10
3 3 4

Sample Output 4

0

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Maybe there is a dynamic programming over "deficit" using the given X as a "barrier".

Let's consider automaton of subsequences. For a word A, the set of absent length M words can be represented by the "failure positions" for each prefix? There is a known concept: The "subsequence automaton" of A has states as positions 0..N, transition from i by letter c to next occurrence of c after i. A word W is a subsequence iff starting at 0, following transitions, reaches <=N. The set of absent length M words are those paths that fail at some state. We need exactly one failing path X. Count A such that the subsequence automaton has a unique path of length M that fails, and that path is X.

This can be viewed as: In the complete K-ary tree of depth M, all nodes except leaf X are present (i.e., correspond to subsequences). A leaf Y present iff there is a path from root to Y in the automaton. The automaton is deterministic with N+1 states (positions). We need the set of leaves reached by all words except X.

Maybe count sequences A by considering the "next occurrence" functions. For a given A, define for each position i (0..N) and letter c, nxt[i][c] = first position > i where c occurs, or ∞. Then a word W is absent iff applying nxt from 0 fails (∞) before length M. For X absent, the path for X reaches ∞ at step M: nxt[... nxt[0][X_1] ...][X_M] = ∞. For every other word, path succeeds.

Condition C:
- E(U) exists.
- For all Y != U, E(Y) < L <= E(U)? Wait need L <= E(U) for X absent? If L <= E(U), then no X_M after E(U), so U + X_M = X absent. If L > E(U), then X_M occurs after E(U), so X present, invalid. 2. For all c != X_M, L_c > E(U). (So E(U) < min_{c != X_M} L_c)

Can combine: max_{Y != U} E(Y) < L <= E(U) < min_{c != X_M} L_c.

Is this sufficient for all length M words present except X?
Take any length M word W.
If W = U + c:
- If c = X_M: absent because L <= E(U) means no X_M after E(U). Good.
- If c != X_M: present because L_c > E(U), so choose occurrence of c after E(U). Good.
If W = Y + c with Y != U (length M-1 prefix):
- Since E(Y) < L <= L_c? For c = X_M, L_c = L > E(Y), so X_M after E(Y). For c != X_M, L_c > E(U) >= L > E(Y)? Wait L_c > E(U) and E(U) >= L, so L_c > E(Y). Thus c occurs after E(Y). So W present. Good.
Thus condition is sufficient.

So the problem reduces to counting A of length N over K such that:
- A contains all length M-1 words (universality index at least M-1). Actually if E(Y) defined for all Y length M-1. Since M-1 <= N? M<=N. Need all length M-1 subsequences present. Now how to count sequences A satisfying this condition? This is still complex but maybe manageable with DP over "ranks" of positions.

Observe E(Y) can be computed by a DP over positions:

This recursive threshold condition might lead to a counting method using DP over positions and "last occurrences" of letters.

Let's define the arch decomposition more concretely. Let t_0=0. For i=1..M-1:

Maybe we can compute s_i from the arch decomposition? There is a recurrence for s_i. Let's derive.

For a word A with unique max word U_i = X[1..i] at level i, let t_i = E(U_i), and s_i = max_{Y != U_i} E(Y). We want to compute s_i in terms of previous positions and thresholds.

Given t_i and p_{i+1}? Let's derive recurrence for s_i.

For length i, any word Y = Z c. E(Y) = nxt(E(Z), c). The maximum over Y != U_i. We know U_i = U_{i-1} X_i has E=t_i. For other words:

At arch i, before building it, we have s_{i-1} from previous. We need p_i = last X_i up to t_{i-1} > s_{i-1}. This p_i is known from previous prefix. So when building previous arches, we must have ensured that the last occurrence of X_i (which is the next arch's completion letter) is after s_{i-1}? Actually s_{i-1} is computed after arch i-1, so p_i is also after arch i-1. The condition is between two quantities both known after arch i-1. Thus after completing arch i-1, we can check if l_{i-1}(X_i) > s_{i-1}. If yes, arch i can proceed. This means when constructing arch i-1, we need to know X_i and ensure the last occurrence of X_i in the prefix up to t_{i-1} is > s_{i-1}. Since s_{i-1} depends on r_{i-1} and previous, this is a constraint on the content of arch i-1.

This suggests a DP over i with state (t_i, s_i, maybe last occurrence of next letter?). But the next letter X_{i+1} is fixed, so after arch i we need know l_i(X_{i+1}) to check next condition. If X_{i+1} = X_i, l_i = t_i. If X_{i+1} != X_i, l_i is the last occurrence of X_{i+1} in P_i. This is a single value, not all letters. However, for the arch after next, we will need l_{i+1}(X_{i+2}), which may be last occurrence of X_{i+2} in P_{i+1} (or t_{i+1}). So at each step, we only need the last occurrence of the next completion letter at the current boundary. But to construct P_i, we need to know the last occurrence of X_{i+1} in P_i. That is determined by where X_{i+1} appears in P_i. We can count choices of P_i with a specified last occurrence position q = l_i(X_{i+1}) (if X_{i+1} != X_i; if equal q=t_i). Also we need r_i and t_i.

Maybe we can count arch i independently given:
- start position a = t_{i-1}
- previous s = s_{i-1}
- current completion letter x = X_i
- next completion letter y = X_{i+1} (for i < M-1) or final missing letter z = X_M (for i = M-1? Actually after last arch, we need L = l_{M-1}(z) > s_{M-1}; so we need last occurrence of z at boundary M-1. z may be any letter, possibly equal to X_{M-1} or not. If z = X_{M-1}, then L=t_{M-1}; if not, L is last occurrence of z in P_{M-1}.)
- We need choose a word P_i of length len = t_i - a (including final x at t_i) such that:
   * positions a+1..t_i-1 contain no x, and contain all letters except x at least once.
   * Let r = max first occurrence position among letters != x within P_i. (If K-1 letters, the last new letter among them.)
   * Let s_new = max(r, nxt(s, x)) for i>=2; for i=1 s_new = r. Here nxt(s, x) is the first occurrence of x after position s in the prefix up to t_i. Since x does not appear in P_i before t_i, nxt(s,x) is either some occurrence of x in previous prefix after s, or t_i if no x after s before t_i. This value is determined by previous prefix and t_i? Actually if s < p_i (where p_i = last x before t_i), then there is at least one x after s; nxt(s,x) is the first such, which is known from previous prefix, not from P_i. If s >= p_i, invalid. So nxt(s,x) is a known value b (the first x after s in the prefix before arch i). It does not depend on P_i. Good.
   * The last occurrence of y (next letter) at boundary t_i, call q. If y = x, q = t_i. If y != x, q is the last occurrence of y in P_i (positions a+1..t_i-1), because y != x and P_i contains y. This q must be > s_new (for next condition) if i < M-1; for final, q = L must be > s_new and <= t_i.
   * Also r < t_i (since x unique max at this arch). And for i=1, need r < t_1 (x unique first max).
   * Additionally, t_i is the first occurrence of x after a, so x does not appear in (a, t_i). Since P_i before final has no x, okay.

Thus the arch content can be summarized by:
- length Larch = t_i - a (>= K, because need K-1 other letters plus final x).
- r = position (relative to a) of the last first occurrence among letters != x.
- q = last occurrence of y (if y != x) relative to a; if y=x, q=t_i.
- The actual word P_i over alphabet excluding x, of length Larch-1, containing all K-1 letters, with:
   * The set of letters first appears completely at relative position r (i.e., r is the maximum of first occurrence positions).
   * The last occurrence of y is at relative position q (if y != x).
   * No other constraints? Also the final x at relative Larch.

But wait, the first occurrence of x after s (b) might be after a? No, x does not appear in P_i before final, so b is either in prefix <=a or t_i. If s < p_i, b <= p_i <= a. So b is in prefix. Thus s_new = max(r, b). Since b is known and < a? Actually p_i <= a, b <= p_i <= a. r is in (a, t_i). So s_new = r if r > b, else b. Since r > a >= b? b <= a, r > a, so r > b. Therefore s_new = r! Wait for i>=2, b = nxt(s, x) is the first x after s. Since s < p_i <= a, and r > a, we have b <= a < r. Thus s_new = max(r, b) = r. Is that always? What if p_i = a and b = a? r > a, so yes r > b. Therefore s_i = r_i for all i>=1! Let's verify with valid K=2 M=3: i=2, r2=3, s2=3 yes. Invalid A: i=2, r2=5, but earlier we computed s2=6 due to nxt(s1=2, X2=2)=6. Wait r2 was max_{c!=2} nxt(3,c) = max(4,5)=5. But s2 we computed as max(r2=5, nxt(s1=2,2)=6) =6. Here b=6, which is > r2. But p2 = last 2 before t2 = 1, s1=2, condition s1 < p2? 2<1 false. Since condition failed, b = nxt(2,2)=6 = t_i, not <= a. Because s >= p_i, so there is no x after s before t_i; the first x after s is t_i. Thus b=t_i > r. In valid cases, condition s < p_i ensures there is an x after s before t_i, so b <= p_i <= a < r, hence s_i = r_i. Great! So if the uniqueness condition holds, s_i = r_i. This is a huge simplification.

Let's prove: For i>=2, if s_{i-1} < p_i (last x before t_i), then nxt(s_{i-1}, x) <= p_i <= t_{i-1} < r_i? Wait r_i is in arch i, so r_i > t_{i-1}. Thus b <= t_{i-1} < r_i. Therefore s_i = max(r_i, b) = r_i. For i=1, s_1 = r_1. Thus by induction, as long as all conditions hold, s_i = r_i for all i. Then the condition for next arch s_i < p_{i+1} becomes r_i < p_{i+1}. And final condition s_{M-1} < L becomes r_{M-1} < L.

This is much simpler! Let's verify with valid K=2 M=3 X=111:
i=1: r1=1 (first occurrence of 2), s1=1.
i=2: p2=last 1 before t2 = t1=2. Condition r1=1 < p2=2 holds. r2=3, s2=3. Final L=last 1 =4, condition r2=3 < L=4 holds. Good.

Invalid A: i=1 r1=2, s1=2. i=2 p2=last 2 before t2=1. Condition r1=2 < p2=1 fails. So invalid.

Thus the entire uniqueness condition reduces to:
- For each arch i=1..M-1, let r_i be the position (global) of the last first occurrence among letters other than X_i within arch i (i.e., the position where the set of letters seen in arch i, excluding X_i, first becomes all K-1). Then r_i < t_i (automatically).
- For i=1..M-2, require r_i < p_{i+1}, where p_{i+1} is the last occurrence of X_{i+1} before t_{i+1} (which is the last occurrence of X_{i+1} up to t_i, since X_{i+1} not in arch i+1 before final). Equivalently, the last occurrence of X_{i+1} in the prefix up to t_i must be after r_i.
- For final, require r_{M-1} < L, where L is the last occurrence of X_M up to t_{M-1} (since suffix has no X_M). Equivalently, the last occurrence of X_M in the prefix up to t_{M-1} must be after r_{M-1}.
- Also for i=1, r_1 < t_1? Since r_1 is the last first occurrence among letters != X1; t1 is first X1. If r_1 < t1, then X1 is the unique letter with first occurrence at t1. This is equivalent to p? For i=1, no previous r. We need r_1 < t_1. But since t1 is after all first occurrences of other letters, yes by definition r_1 < t1 if X1 is the last letter to first appear. If some other letter first appears at t1? Impossible because t1 position is X1. So r_1 < t1 always? The maximum first occurrence among other letters could be at t1? No, t1 is X1, so other letters cannot first appear at t1. Thus r_1 < t1 automatically. However, if another letter first appears after t1? Then arch1 would not be complete at t1. But arch1 is defined as first complete set; so all letters appear by t1, and t1 is the first position where all letters have appeared. Since t1 is X1, all other letters appear before t1. So r_1 < t1 automatic. Good.

Now, what about p_{i+1}? Since X_{i+1} may equal X_i or not. If X_{i+1} = X_i, then p_{i+1} = t_i (because arch i ends with X_i). Then condition r_i < t_i is always true. If X_{i+1} != X_i, then p_{i+1} is the last occurrence of X_{i+1} within arch i (before final X_i), because arch i contains all letters except X_i, so it contains X_{i+1}. Thus condition r_i < last occurrence of X_{i+1} in P_i. This means the letter X_{i+1} must appear in arch i after the position r_i where all other letters have first appeared. In other words, after the arch i has seen all letters except X_i, it must later see X_{i+1} (unless X_{i+1}=X_i, where the final X_i at t_i serves as p_{i+1}? Wait if X_{i+1}=X_i, p_{i+1}=t_i, and condition r_i < t_i true. So no need for X_i inside P_i after r_i; the final X_i is after r_i anyway.)
For final, if X_M = X_{M-1}, then L = t_{M-1}, condition r_{M-1} < t_{M-1} true. If X_M != X_{M-1}, then L is the last occurrence of X_M in P_{M-1}, and we need r_{M-1} < L. So same condition: the final missing letter X_M must appear in the last arch after r_{M-1}, unless it equals X_{M-1} (then final X_{M-1} suffices).

This is very elegant! Let's restate.

We have M-1 arches. For arch i:
- It is a word over alphabet K \ {X_i} that contains all those letters, followed by X_i.
- Let r_i be the global position of the last "new" letter among K\{X_i} within this arch (i.e., the position where the set of letters seen in this arch becomes exactly all letters except X_i). After r_i, the arch may contain repetitions of letters from K\{X_i}, and finally X_i at t_i.
- For each i from 1 to M-2, if X_{i+1} != X_i, then X_{i+1} must occur in arch i after r_i. If X_{i+1} = X_i, automatically satisfied by final X_i at t_i.
- For final, if X_M != X_{M-1}, then X_M must occur in arch M-1 after r_{M-1}; if X_M = X_{M-1}, automatically satisfied.
- Additionally, the suffix R after t_{M-1} must contain all letters except X_M and no X_M.

Is this sufficient for uniqueness? Let's test with earlier invalid A=2 3 1 1 3 2 1 2, X=123.
Arches: P1=2 3 1, X1=1. K\{1}={2,3}. First occurrences: 2 at1,3 at2, r1=2. Next X2=2 !=1. Need 2 occur in arch1 after r1=2. Arch1 positions 1..3: 2 at1,3 at2,1 at3; no 2 after r1. Fails. Good.

What about a case where X_{i+1} appears after r_i but before final X_i? Example construct valid for X=123? Need arch1 P1 with X1=1, r1 maybe 2, and X2=2 after r1. Suppose P1 = 3 2 1? First occurrences: 3 at1,2 at2, r1=2, X2=2 at r1 not after; condition r1 < last occurrence of 2? last 2=2, not > r1. Need 2 after r1, e.g. P1 = 3 2 3 1? Thus the arch condition may be sufficient.

Now, counting such A reduces to counting sequences of arches with constraints on the position r_i and the last occurrence of the next letter after r_i. This is now tractable with DP over positions and maybe only r_i.

Let's formalize arch i in relative terms.

Let alphabet size K. For each arch i, the completion letter is x = X_i (for i=1..M-1). Let S = [K] \ {x}, size K-1. The arch body B_i is a word over S of length l_i >= K-1 that contains every letter of S at least once. Then arch i is B_i followed by x. Let a = t_{i-1} (global start), b = t_i = a + l_i + 1 (global end). Let r_i be the global position of the last first occurrence of letters of S in B_i. Equivalently, in relative position within B_i, let ρ_i = r_i - a (1 <= ρ_i <= l_i) be the position where the set of distinct letters in B_i first becomes S. Since B_i contains all S, ρ_i is the maximum over c in S of the first occurrence position of c in B_i. After ρ_i, B_i may have arbitrary letters from S.

The condition involving next letter y = X_{i+1} (or final X_M):
- If y == x, then no additional constraint on B_i beyond ρ_i < l_i+1? Actually final x at global t_i = a+l_i+1, so r_i = a+ρ_i < a+l_i+1 = t_i automatically. Good.
- If y != x, then y ∈ S. We need the last occurrence of y in B_i to be after ρ_i. That is, y appears at least once in the suffix of B_i after position ρ_i. Let q_i = last occurrence position of y in B_i (relative). Need q_i > ρ_i.

Additionally, for i < M-1, the next arch's start is t_i. The condition for arch i+1 will involve r_i? Actually we already used condition r_i < p_{i+1}. p_{i+1} is last occurrence of X_{i+1} up to t_i. If y != x, p_{i+1} = a + q_i (last y in B_i). If y = x, p_{i+1} = t_i = a+l_i+1. So condition is exactly q_i > ρ_i or l_i+1 > ρ_i. Good.

Now, the suffix R after the last arch:
- It is a word over T = [K] \ {X_M} of length N - t_{M-1} that contains every letter of T at least once. No X_M. This is independent of prefix except length.

Thus the counting problem decomposes into:
1. Count the number of ways to choose the sequence of arch bodies B_1,...,B_{M-1} (over alphabets depending on X_i) with lengths l_i >= K-1, total length of bodies plus final letters = T = t_{M-1} <= N, such that for each i:
   - B_i contains all letters in S_i = [K]\{X_i}.
   - Let ρ_i be the position of the last first occurrence of letters in S_i within B_i (1..l_i).
   - Let y_i = X_{i+1} for i=1..M-2, and y_{M-1} = X_M.
   - If y_i == X_i, no further constraint; else y_i ∈ S_i and the last occurrence of y_i in B_i is after ρ_i.
   Additionally, the total prefix length T = sum_i (l_i + 1) can be from (M-1)*K to N. The suffix length is N-T and must be at least K-1? Actually suffix must contain K-1 distinct letters, so length >= K-1. Thus T <= N - (K-1). Also T >= K*(M-1). If not, answer 0.
2. Multiply by number of suffixes of length N-T over K-1 letters containing all letters.

Now, how to count arch bodies B_i with the constraint? This is a combinatorial counting problem over words of length l over alphabet of size K-1 (the allowed letters S_i), with a distinguished "next letter" y (which may be absent if y==X_i? If y==X_i, then y is not in S_i; constraint is none. But in that case the next completion letter equals current, so y is the final x, not in body. The body alphabet S_i excludes x. So no distinguished letter in body. If y != x, y is one of the K-1 letters in S_i; we need the last occurrence of y after the position where all letters have appeared.)

Let's define for a word B over alphabet S of size d = K-1, containing all d letters:
- ρ = max_{c in S} first_pos(c) = the position where the last new letter appears.
- For a specified letter y in S, let last_y = last position of y. Constraint: last_y > ρ.

We need count words of length l over S containing all letters, with this constraint, for each arch. Since the alphabets S_i differ only by which letter is excluded; by symmetry, the count depends only on d=K-1 and whether the distinguished y is in S (i.e., y != X_i) or not. If y == X_i, no distinguished letter; count of all words of length l over S containing all letters. If y != X_i, count of words over S containing all letters and with last occurrence of specified y after the last first occurrence of all letters.

Because the alphabet size is K-1, and the specified letter is just one of them. By symmetry, the count for y != X_i is the same for any y in S. Let's denote:
- A_d(l) = number of words of length l over a d-letter alphabet that contain all d letters at least once.
- B_d(l) = number of such words where additionally a specified letter y has its last occurrence after the position ρ (the last first occurrence of all letters). Equivalently, y appears at least once after the moment when all d letters have appeared.

We need compute these for d = K-1, l up to N (<=400). Then the total count for prefix is sum over lengths l_1..l_{M-1} >= d, total L = sum l_i, T = L + (M-1) <= N - (K-1), of product over i of weight_i(l_i), where weight_i(l) = A_d(l) if X_{i+1} == X_i (or for i=M-1 if X_M == X_{M-1}), else B_d(l) if X_{i+1} != X_i. Wait for i=M-1, y = X_M; if X_M == X_{M-1}, no constraint, weight A_d(l); if X_M != X_{M-1}, weight B_d(l). For i<M-1, same.

Is that all? Let's verify with sample1: N=5,M=2,K=3,d=2. X=1,1. M-1=1 arch. For i=1, y=X_M=1, X_i=1, equal, weight A_2(l_1). l_1 >= d=2. T = l_1+1. Need suffix length N-T over alphabet K\{X_M}={2,3} containing both letters. N=5. Possible l_1? T<=N-(K-1)=5-2=3, so l_1+1<=3 => l_1<=2. l_1>=2, so l_1=2. A_2(2)=2! (words over {2,3} containing both: 23,32). Suffix length = N-T = 5-3=2, number of suffixes over {2,3} containing both =2. Product=4. Matches sample1.

Sample4: N=29,M=3,K=10,X=3,3,4. d=9. M-1=2 arches. X1=3,X2=3 (equal) weight A_9(l1). X2=3, X3=4 (not equal) weight B_9(l2). Suffix over K\{4} size9 containing all. Need total T = l1+l2+2 <= N-(K-1)=29-9=20. l1,l2>=9. Minimum T=20, so l1=l2=9. Count = A_9(9)*B_9(9)*suffix_length? suffix length = N-T =9, suffix count = A_9(9) (over 9 letters containing all). So answer = A_9(9)^2 * B_9(9)? Wait suffix count = A_9(9) (since suffix alphabet size9, length9 containing all = 9!). A_9(9)=9! = 362880. B_9(9) for specified y: words length9 over 9 letters containing all, and last y after ρ. Since length equals alphabet size, each letter appears exactly once. ρ is position of the last letter in the permutation. last_y is position of y. Constraint last_y > ρ means y appears after the last letter? Impossible because ρ is the maximum position among all letters, so no position after ρ. Thus B_9(9)=0. Answer 0. Matches sample4. Great!

Sample3: N=29,M=3,K=10,X=3,3,3. d=9. X1=3,X2=3 equal weight A_9(l1). X2=3, X3=3 equal weight A_9(l2). Suffix over K\{3} size9 containing all. Minimum T=20, l1=l2=9. Answer = A_9(9)^2 * A_9(9) = (9!)^3? 362880^3 mod 998244353. Let's check sample output 495293602. Compute? 362880 mod 998244353. 362880^2 = 131681894400? mod. Let's quick approximate maybe output? We'll trust. Could be.

Sample2: N=400,M=3,K=9,X=1,8,6. d=8. X1=1,X2=8 not equal weight B_8(l1). X2=8,X3=6 not equal weight B_8(l2). Suffix over K\{6} size8 containing all. Need l1,l2>=8, T=l1+l2+2 <= N-(K-1)=400-8=392. Many lengths. Answer = sum_{l1,l2>=8, l1+l2<=392} B_8(l1) B_8(l2) * suffix_count(392 - l1 - l2? Wait N-T = 400 - (l1+l2+2) = 398 - (l1+l2). Suffix alphabet size d=8, length s = 398 - l1 - l2. Need s>=8. So l1+l2 <=390? Let's compute T<=N-(K-1)=400-8=392 => l1+l2+2 <=392 => l1+l2 <=390. Suffix length = N-T = 400 - (l1+l2+2) = 398 - (l1+l2). If l1+l2=390, suffix length=8. Good. Need sum. We'll compute later maybe.

So the problem reduces to computing A_d(l) and B_d(l) for d=K-1 up to 399, l up to N (400), and then a convolution over M-1 arches with weights depending on equalities.

Let's derive formulas for A_d(l) and B_d(l).

A_d(l): number of words of length l over d letters using all d letters at least once.
A_d(l) = d! * S(l,d) = sum_{j=0}^d (-1)^j C(d,j) (d-j)^l.
Equivalently inclusion-exclusion.

B_d(l): number of words of length l over d letters containing all d letters, and for a specified letter y, last_y > ρ, where ρ = max first occurrence positions of all letters.

We need count this. Let's analyze.

We have d letters, one distinguished y. A word B of length l over alphabet S, all letters appear. Let ρ = position where the last new letter first appears. Let last_y = position of last occurrence of y. Constraint last_y > ρ.

Since y is one of the letters, its first occurrence f_y <= ρ (because ρ is max of all first occurrences, so f_y <= ρ). Its last occurrence l_y >= f_y. Constraint l_y > ρ means y occurs at least once after ρ. Note that after ρ, all letters have appeared at least once; the suffix of B from ρ+1 to l can contain any letters. The condition is that y appears in the suffix after ρ.

We can count B_d(l) by considering the position ρ and the prefix up to ρ.

At position ρ, the set of letters seen in prefix B[1..ρ] becomes all d letters for the first time. Thus:
- The prefix of length ρ contains all d letters.
- The letter at position ρ is some letter c (the last new letter). Before ρ, the prefix contains all letters except c; c appears for the first time at ρ. So c is the "last new letter".
- For any letter a != c, its first occurrence is before ρ; for c, first occurrence = ρ.
- The prefix before ρ (length ρ-1) contains exactly d-1 distinct letters (all except c), and does not contain c.

The suffix after ρ (positions ρ+1..l) is arbitrary over all d letters, but must satisfy the constraint if y appears in suffix. Also note y could be c or not.

We need count over all choices of c (the last new letter) and words.

Case 1: y = c (the last new letter). Then y's first occurrence is at ρ. For last_y > ρ, we need y to appear at least once in the suffix after ρ. The prefix before ρ is a word over d-1 letters (all except y) containing all of them, of length ρ-1. Number of such prefixes: A_{d-1}(ρ-1). The suffix of length l-ρ over d letters must contain y at least once. Number: d^{l-ρ} - (d-1)^{l-ρ}. The letter at ρ is fixed y. A_d(l) = number of surjections from l positions to d labels.
For fixed d, we can compute for l=0..N:

If first l-1 already contain all d, last can be any of d: d * A_d(l-1). If first l-1 contain exactly d-1 letters (missing the last letter), then last letter must be the missing one, and the first l-1 is a surjection onto a specific set of d-1 letters. Number of first l-1 containing exactly a fixed set of d-1 letters? That's not simply A_{d-1}(l-1) because it must not contain the missing letter; but if alphabet is those d-1 letters, A_{d-1}(l-1) counts words using all d-1 letters and no others. However, B_d(l) formula uses A_{d-1}(m) for m up to l-1. We can compute B_d(l) by iterating ρ:

This is O(N^2) for fixed d (N<=400) fine. We can optimize with convolution but not needed.

Now, let's prove the arch condition more formally to ensure no missing constraints.

We defined r_i = position where arch i first contains all letters except X_i. We claimed s_i = r_i if previous condition holds. Let's prove by induction.

Let U_i = X_1..X_i. Let t_i be end of arch i. Let E(W) be earliest end position. We know t_i = max_{|W|=i} E(W) and U_i achieves t_i if conditions hold.

Base i=1: Arch1 is minimal complete word ending X1. Since X1 is the last new letter in the whole prefix, any other single letter first appears before t1. Thus U_1 is unique max, s_1 = r_1 (max first occurrence among other letters). Good.

Inductive step: Suppose U_{i-1} unique max with E=t_{i-1}, s_{i-1}=r_{i-1}. For arch i, since X_i does not appear in body before t_i, and arch i body contains all other letters, t_i = max_c nxt(t_{i-1}, c) with unique X_i attaining t_i. Thus any max word of length i must end with X_i. A word Z X_i has E = nxt(E(Z), X_i). It equals t_i iff E(Z) >= p_i, where p_i is last occurrence of X_i before t_i (because no X_i between p_i and t_i). Since U_{i-1} has E=t_{i-1} >= p_i, U_i is max. Other Z have E(Z) <= s_{i-1}=r_{i-1}. If r_{i-1} < p_i, then no other Z has E>=p_i, so U_i unique max. Also s_i = max over non-U_i words. For words ending with c != X_i, max E is r_i (the last first occurrence of c after t_{i-1}? Actually any word Z c with c != X_i: E <= nxt(t_{i-1}, c) <= r_i, and there is a single-letter? Wait for length i, to achieve r_i, choose Z=U_{i-1}? If c != X_i, nxt(t_{i-1}, c) is the first occurrence of c after t_{i-1}, which is <= r_i. But is there a word of length i with E = r_i? Need prefix Z of length i-1 with E(Z) <= t_{i-1} and nxt(E(Z), c) = r_i. Since r_i is the first occurrence of some letter c after t_{i-1}? Actually r_i is max over c != X_i of nxt(t_{i-1}, c). Let c* be a letter with nxt(t_{i-1}, c*) = r_i. Then U_{i-1} c* has E = r_i (because E(U_{i-1})=t_{i-1}). So max for c != X_i is r_i. For words ending with X_i and Z != U_{i-1}, E = nxt(E(Z), X_i) <= nxt(s_{i-1}, X_i) <= p_i? Wait if s_{i-1}=r_{i-1} < p_i, then nxt(s_{i-1}, X_i) is some occurrence <= p_i. But r_i > t_{i-1} >= p_i? Actually p_i <= t_{i-1} < r_i, so r_i > p_i >= nxt(...). Thus s_i = r_i. Good. So induction holds.

Thus the condition r_{i-1} < p_i for i>=2 is necessary and sufficient. And p_i is last occurrence of X_i before t_i. If X_i = X_{i-1}, then p_i = t_{i-1} (since arch i-1 ended with X_{i-1}=X_i), and r_{i-1} < t_{i-1} always. If X_i != X_{i-1}, then p_i is last occurrence of X_i in body of arch i-1 (since body contains X_i). So condition r_{i-1} < last occurrence of X_i in body i-1. This is exactly our B_d constraint for arch i-1 with y = X_i. For i=1, no previous condition; but we also need U_1 unique max, which is automatic if arch1 is minimal ending X1? Actually if X1 appears earlier in prefix? Arch1 is from start, and X1 is the last new letter. Since X1 does not appear before t1, yes. If X1 appeared before t1, then t1 wouldn't be first X1? Wait arch1 is the shortest prefix containing all letters. The last letter of arch1 is the letter that completes the set. It could be a letter that appeared earlier? No, if it appeared earlier, the set would already be complete before that position. Thus the completing letter appears for the first time at t1. So X1 is unique max. Good.

For final absent X, we need after matching U_{M-1} at t_{M-1}, no X_M after, but all other letters after. This is equivalent to L = last X_M <= t_{M-1} and L_c > t_{M-1} for c != X_M. The suffix R must contain all c != X_M. We enforce suffix count. Also need U_{M-1} unique max and s_{M-1}=r_{M-1} < L. If X_M = X_{M-1}, L = t_{M-1} (because arch M-1 ends with X_M), and r_{M-1} < t_{M-1} automatic. If X_M != X_{M-1}, L is last occurrence of X_M in body M-1, and condition r_{M-1} < L is exactly B_d constraint for last arch. Then for any word Y != U, E(Y) <= r_{M-1} < L, so Y+X_M present (X_M occurs after E(Y) at L). For Y=U, E=t_{M-1} >= L, so X absent. For any c != X_M, since suffix contains c after t_{M-1}, U+c present. For Y != U and c != X_M, c occurs after E(Y) because suffix after t_{M-1} contains c and E(Y) <= r_{M-1} < L <= t_{M-1}? Actually if E(Y) could be > t_{M-1}? No, max E is t_{M-1}, so E(Y) <= t_{M-1}; suffix after t_{M-1} has c, so c after E(Y). Thus all other words present. Good.

Thus formula is solid.

A[0][0] = 1
A[0][l>0] = 0
For s from 1 to d:
  A[s][0] = 0
  for l from 1 to N:
     A[s][l] = s * (A[s][l-1] + A[s-1][l-1]) mod MOD
Let's verify for s=1: A[1][l]=1*(A[1][l-1]+A[0][l-1]). A[1][0]=0. l=1: 0+1=1. l=2:1+0=1. Good.
For s=2: A[2][1]=2*(0+0)=0. A[2][2]=2*(0 + A[1][1]=1)=2. Words over 2 letters length2 containing both: 2. Good.
This recurrence is efficient O(d*N).

Now, let's derive B_d(l) formula more rigorously to ensure no double counting.

We count words B of length l over alphabet S (size d), all letters appear. Let ρ be the position of the last first occurrence. Let c be the letter at position ρ. Then:
- c appears for the first time at ρ.
- The prefix B[1..ρ-1] is a word over S\{c} that contains all letters of S\{c}. Number of such prefixes: A_{d-1}(ρ-1). For ρ=1, this requires d-1=0, i.e., d=1, and A_0(0)=1.
- The suffix B[ρ+1..l] is any word over S (length l-ρ).
- For the specified y, constraint last_y > ρ means y appears in the suffix. Number of suffixes containing y: total d^{l-ρ} minus suffixes avoiding y: (d-1)^{l-ρ}.
- There are d choices for c. If c=y, this is included; if c != y, also included. The count of prefixes A_{d-1}(ρ-1) is independent of which c, because alphabet S\{c} has size d-1 and contains y if c!=y, or not if c=y. Does A_{d-1}(ρ-1) count words over S\{c} containing all d-1 letters. If c=y, S\{c} does not contain y; if c!=y, it contains y. But the number of words over a specific set of d-1 letters containing all of them is the same A_{d-1}(ρ-1). So multiply by d. Good.
Thus formula correct.

     if pref == 0: continue

  B[l] = d * total % MOD

Potential issue: A_d(l) recurrence A[s][l] = s*(A[s][l-1]+A[s-1][l-1]) for s up to d. For s=0, A[0][0]=1. For s>0, A[s][0]=0. This recurrence counts words of length l over s letters using all s. Let's prove: Consider last position. If the first l-1 positions already contain all s letters, last can be any of s: s*A[s][l-1]. If the first l-1 positions contain exactly s-1 letters, missing one letter, last must be that missing letter. For each missing letter, first l-1 is a word over the other s-1 letters using all of them: A[s-1][l-1]. There are s choices. This counts all words exactly once because the first l-1 either contains all s or not. If it contains fewer than s-1, cannot become all s by adding one letter. Good.

Now, let's think about the final answer code structure. We'll write a function solve().

Pseudo:

import sys
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N,M,K = data[:3]
    X = data[3:3+M]
    d = K-1
    if N < M*K - 1:
        print(0)
        return

    # compute A[s][l]
    A = [[0]*(N+1) for _ in range(d+1)]
    A[0][0] = 1
    for s in range(1, d+1):
        As = A[s]
        As_prev = A[s-1]
        for l in range(1, N+1):
            As[l] = s * (As[l-1] + As_prev[l-1]) % MOD

    # powers
    pow_d = [1]*(N+1)
    pow_dm1 = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        pow_d[i] = pow_d[i-1] * d % MOD
        pow_dm1[i] = pow_dm1[i-1] * (d-1) % MOD

    # B
    B = [0]*(N+1)
    A_dm1 = A[d-1]  # if d>=1
    for l in range(1, N+1):
        total = 0
        for rho in range(1, l+1):
            pref = A_dm1[rho-1]
            if pref == 0:
                continue
            diff = pow_d[l-rho] - pow_dm1[l-rho]
            if diff:
                total = (total + pref * diff) % MOD
        B[l] = d * total % MOD

    # weights
    weights = []
    for i in range(M-1):
        if X[i+1] == X[i]:
            weights.append(A[d])
        else:
            weights.append(B)

    maxL = N - (M-1) - d
    if maxL < 0:
        print(0)
        return

    dp = [0]*(maxL+1)
    dp[0] = 1
    for w in weights:
        ndp = [0]*(maxL+1)
        # local variables for speed
        for curL, val in enumerate(dp):
            if val == 0:
                continue
            limit = maxL - curL
            # l from d to limit (since weights zero below d)
            # But if d > limit, no l
            for l in range(d, limit+1):
                wl = w[l]
                if wl:
                    ndp[curL+l] = (ndp[curL+l] + val * wl) % MOD
        dp = ndp

    ans = 0
    Ad = A[d]
    for L, val in enumerate(dp):
        if val == 0:
            continue
        s = N - (L + M - 1)
        if s >= 0:
            ans = (ans + val * Ad[s]) % MOD
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

Now, let's think about if M*K - 1 could overflow? Python no.

Before final, let's maybe write the code cleanly:

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M, K = data[0], data[1], data[2]
    X = data[3:3+M]
    d = K - 1

    if N < M * K - 1:
        print(0)
        return

    # A[s][l] = number of words of length l over s letters using all s letters
    A = [[0]*(N+1) for _ in range(d+1)]
    A[0][0] = 1
    for s in range(1, d+1):
        As = A[s]
        prev = A[s-1]
        for l in range(1, N+1):
            As[l] = s * (As[l-1] + prev[l-1]) % MOD

    pow_d = [1]*(N+1)
    pow_dm1 = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        pow_d[i] = pow_d[i-1] * d % MOD
        pow_dm1[i] = pow_dm1[i-1] * (d-1) % MOD

    diff_pow = [(pow_d[i] - pow_dm1[i]) % MOD for i in range(N+1)]

    # B[l] = words over d letters containing all, and specified letter's last occurrence is after the last first occurrence
    B = [0]*(N+1)
    A_dm1 = A[d-1]
    for l in range(1, N+1):
        total = 0
        for rho in range(1, l+1):
            pref = A_dm1[rho-1]
            if pref:
                total = (total + pref * diff_pow[l-rho]) % MOD
        B[l] = d * total % MOD

    Ad = A[d]
    weights = []
    for i in range(M-1):
        if X[i+1] == X[i]:
            weights.append(Ad)
        else:
            weights.append(B)

    maxL = N - (M - 1) - d
    if maxL < 0:
        print(0)
        return

    dp = [0]*(maxL+1)
    dp[0] = 1
    for w in weights:
        ndp = [0]*(maxL+1)
        for curL, val in enumerate(dp):
            if val == 0:
                continue
            limit = maxL - curL
            for l in range(d, limit+1):
                wl = w[l]
                if wl:
                    ndp[curL+l] = (ndp[curL+l] + val * wl) % MOD
        dp = ndp

    ans = 0
    for L, val in enumerate(dp):
        if val == 0:
            continue
        s = N - (L + M - 1)
        if s >= 0:
            ans = (ans + val * Ad[s]) % MOD

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Potential issue: The weights `Ad` and `B` are lists of length N+1. In DP, `w[l]` with l up to maxL <= N. Good.

Potential issue: If `maxL` is large but `d` is large, range(d, limit+1) might be empty for early curL, but for curL=0 limit=maxL >= d. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N, M, K = data[0], data[1], data[2]
    X = data[3:3 + M]

    d = K - 1

    # Minimum possible length: (M-1) arches of length K + suffix of length K-1
    if N < M * K - 1:
        print(0)
        return

    # A[s][l] = number of words of length l over s letters using all s letters
    A = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(d + 1)]
    A[0][0] = 1
    for s in range(1, d + 1):
        cur = A[s]
        prev = A[s - 1]
        for l in range(1, N + 1):
            cur[l] = s * (cur[l - 1] + prev[l - 1]) % MOD

    pow_d = [1] * (N + 1)
    pow_dm1 = [1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        pow_d[i] = pow_d[i - 1] * d % MOD
        pow_dm1[i] = pow_dm1[i - 1] * (d - 1) % MOD

    diff_pow = [(pow_d[i] - pow_dm1[i]) % MOD for i in range(N + 1)]

    # B[l] = words of length l over d letters using all letters,
    # and a specified letter appears after the last first occurrence.
    B = [0] * (N + 1)
    A_dm1 = A[d - 1]
    for l in range(1, N + 1):
        total = 0
        for rho in range(1, l + 1):
            pref = A_dm1[rho - 1]
            if pref:
                total = (total + pref * diff_pow[l - rho]) % MOD
        B[l] = d * total % MOD

    Ad = A[d]

    # Weight of each arch body.
    # If X[i+1] == X[i], the next completion letter is the same as the current one.
    weights = []
    for i in range(M - 1):
        if X[i + 1] == X[i]:
            weights.append(Ad)
        else:
            weights.append(B)

    maxL = N - (M - 1) - d
    if maxL < 0:
        print(0)
        return

    # DP over total body length of arches.
    dp = [0] * (maxL + 1)
    dp[0] = 1

    for w in weights:
        ndp = [0] * (maxL + 1)
        for curL, val in enumerate(dp):
            if val == 0:
                continue
            limit = maxL - curL
            for l in range(d, limit + 1):
                wl = w[l]
                if wl:
                    ndp[curL + l] = (ndp[curL + l] + val * wl) % MOD
        dp = ndp

    ans = 0
    for L, val in enumerate(dp):
        if val == 0:
            continue
        suffix_len = N - (L + M - 1)
        if suffix_len >= 0:
            ans = (ans + val * Ad[suffix_len]) % MOD

    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
